=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are an infinite amount of bags on a number line, one bag for each coordinate. Some of these bags contain coins.
You are given a 2D array coins, where coins[i] = [l_i, r_i, c_i] denotes that every bag from l_i to r_i contains c_i coins.
The segments that coins contain are non-overlapping.
You are also given an integer k.
Return the maximum amount of coins you can obtain by collecting k consecutive bags.
 
Example 1:

Input: coins = [[8,10,1],[1,3,2],[5,6,4]], k = 4
Output: 10
Explanation:
Selecting bags at positions [3, 4, 5, 6] gives the maximum number of coins: 2 + 0 + 4 + 4 = 10.

Example 2:

Input: coins = [[1,10,3]], k = 2
Output: 6
Explanation:
Selecting bags at positions [1, 2] gives the maximum number of coins: 3 + 3 = 6.

 
Constraints:

1 <= coins.length <= 10^5
1 <= k <= 10^9
coins[i] == [l_i, r_i, c_i]
1 <= l_i <= r_i <= 10^9
1 <= c_i <= 1000
The given segments are non-overlapping.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maximumCoins(self, coins: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

- Loss on left: positions x..x+d-1. Since x>=l_L. If x+d-1 <= r_L, loss d*w_L; if beyond, loss <= d*w_L. Change >= d*w_R - d*w_L >0. So shift right to r_R nondecrease. Good.

Need implement with sorted arrays.

Let's think about bisect and prefix.

Pseudo:

from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left, bisect_right

class Solution:
    def maximumCoins(self, coins: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        coins.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])
        n = len(coins)
        l = [c[0] for c in coins]
        r = [c[1] for c in coins]
        w = [c[2] for c in coins]
        pref = [0]*(n+1)
        for i,(a,b,c) in enumerate(coins):
            pref[i+1] = pref[i] + (b-a+1)*c

        ans = 0
        for i in range(n):
            # start at l_i
            x = l[i]
            y = x + k - 1
            val = self._calc(x,y,l,r,w,pref)
            if val > ans: ans = val

            # start = r_i + 1 - k (end at r_i)
            x = r[i] + 1 - k
            y = x + k - 1  # = r[i]
            val = self._calc(x,y,l,r,w,pref)
            if val > ans: ans = val
        return ans

Need define _calc? Could inline for speed. But final code can have helper method or nested function. LeetCode accepts helper. Need ensure no import issues. Use local variables for speed.

_calc(x,y):
    first = bisect_left(r, x)
    if first == n: return 0
    last = bisect_right(l, y) - 1
    if last < first: return 0
    total = pref[last+1] - pref[first]
    if x > l[first]:
        total -= (x - l[first]) * w[first]
    if y < r[last]:
        total -= (r[last] - y) * w[last]
    return total

Need verify if first==last and x > l[first] and y < r[last], total subtract both. But if window starts before l_i and ends after r_i, no subtract. If window starts before l_i and ends inside, subtract r_i-y. If window starts inside and ends after r_i, subtract x-l_i. If window starts before and ends before l_i? Then first? If y < l_i, last < first? Example x=0,y=5, interval [10,20]. first=bisect_left(r,0)=0, last=bisect_right(l,5)-1 = -1, last<first return0. Good.
If x > r_i, first may be next; if y < l_next, last<first return0.

Need ensure first computed by bisect_left(r,x) works if r list sorted increasing. Since intervals non-overlap and sorted by l, r_i < l_{i+1} <= r_{i+1}? Actually r_i < l_{i+1} <= r_{i+1}, so r_i < r_{i+1}. Yes strictly increasing.

Let's consider memory and code style.

Implementation with helper:

from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left, bisect_right

class Solution:
    def maximumCoins(self, coins: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        coins.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])
        n = len(coins)
        l = [a for a,_,_ in coins]
        r = [b for _,b,_ in coins]
        c = [w for _,_,w in coins]
        pref = [0]*(n+1)
        for i in range(n):
            pref[i+1] = pref[i] + (r[i]-l[i]+1)*c[i]

        def calc(x: int) -> int:
            y = x + k - 1
            first = bisect_left(r, x)
            if first == n:
                return 0
            last = bisect_right(l, y) - 1
            if last < first:
                return 0
            res = pref[last+1] - pref[first]
            if x > l[first]:
                res -= (x - l[first]) * c[first]
            if y < r[last]:
                res -= (r[last] - y) * c[last]
            return res

        ans = 0
        for i in range(n):
            ans = max(ans, calc(l[i]))
            ans = max(ans, calc(r[i] + 1))
            ans = max(ans, calc(l[i] - k))
            ans = max(ans, calc(r[i] + 1 - k))
        return ans

Need ensure `bisect_left(r, x)` uses r list sorted. Since coins sorted by l and non-overlap, r sorted. But if input non-overlapping and sorted by l, r_i < l_{i+1} <= r_{i+1}? Since l_{i+1} <= r_{i+1}; r_i < l_{i+1}; so r_i < r_{i+1}. Good.

Potential issue: `bisect_right(l, y)` returns insertion point after l <= y. If y is huge, last=n-1. Good.

Now, let's maybe derive a proof of breakpoint set to reassure:
Let f(t) be coins at bag t. We want max over x of F(x)=Σ_{t=x}^{x+k-1} f(t). For integer x, F(x+1)-F(x)=f(x+k)-f(x). The function f changes value only at points l_i (from 0 to c_i) and r_i+1 (from c_i to 0). Therefore F(x+1)-F(x) can change only when x+k equals one of those points or x equals one of those points. Thus F is linear (with slope equal to that difference) between consecutive values in set B={l_i, r_i+1, l_i-k, r_i+1-k}. A linear sequence on integers attains its maximum at an endpoint of each interval, so some x in B is optimal. Then compute F(x) for all x in B. This is rigorous. Good.

Now, let's think if "linear" with slope could be non-integer? Difference integer. If slope positive, F increases; if negative, decreases. So max at endpoints. If slope zero, any point. Good.

Let's maybe write final code:

```python
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left, bisect_right

class Solution:
    def maximumCoins(self, coins: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        coins.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])
        n = len(coins)
        l = [c[0] for c in coins]
        r = [c[1] for c in coins]
        w = [c[2] for c in coins]
        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(n):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + (r[i] - l[i] + 1) * w[i]

        bl = bisect_left
        br = bisect_right

        def value(x: int) -> int:
            y = x + k - 1
            first = bl(r, x)
            if first == n:
                return 0
            last = br(l, y) - 1
            if last < first:
                return 0
            ans = pref[last + 1] - pref[first]
            if x > l[first]:
                ans -= (x - l[first]) * w[first]
            if y < r[last]:
                ans -= (r[last] - y) * w[last]
            return ans

        ans = 0
        for i in range(n):
            x = l[i]
            if value(x) > ans: ans = value(x) # This calls twice if not careful.
```
Need avoid calling value twice in condition? We can do:
```
ans = max(ans, value(l[i]))
ans = max(ans, value(r[i] + 1))
ans = max(ans, value(l[i] - k))
ans = max(ans, value(r[i] + 1 - k))
```
This calls once per expression. Good.

Potential micro-optimization: `ans = max(ans, value(l[i]), value(r[i]+1), value(l[i]-k), value(r[i]+1-k))` but this evaluates all anyway. Use separate lines for clarity.

Code:

```python
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left, bisect_right

class Solution:
    def maximumCoins(self, coins: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        coins.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])
        n = len(coins)
        l = [a for a, _, _ in coins]
        r = [b for _, b, _ in coins]
        w = [c for _, _, c in coins]
        
        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(n):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + (r[i] - l[i] + 1) * w[i]
        
        bl = bisect_left
        br = bisect_right
        
        def calc(x: int) -> int:
            y = x + k - 1
            first = bl(r, x)
            if first == n:
                return 0
            last = br(l, y) - 1
            if last < first:
                return 0
            
            res = pref[last + 1] - pref[first]
            if x > l[first]:
                res -= (x - l[first]) * w[first]
            if y < r[last]:
                res -= (r[last] - y) * w[last]
            return res
        
        ans = 0
        for i in range(n):
            ans = max(ans, calc(l[i]))
            ans = max(ans, calc(r[i] + 1))
            ans = max(ans, calc(l[i] - k))
            ans = max(ans, calc(r[i] + 1 - k))
        
        return ans
```

Let's think if `r[i] + 1 - k` could be equal to `l[i] - k`? No, r+1 vs l. Fine.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "calc(r[i] + 1)" for i where r_i+1 equals l_{i+1} if adjacent; duplicate with calc(l[i+1]) but okay.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with "calc(l[i] - k)" for i where l_i-k equals r_{i-1}+1-k if adjacent? Duplicate with calc(r[i-1]+1-k) maybe. okay.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a possible issue with "value" function if `first` and `last` are such that `last + 1` > n? last <= n-1, so last+1 <= n. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with "bisect_right(l, y)" if y is less than l[0], returns 0, last=-1. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a possible issue with "bisect_left(r, x)" if x is less than r[0], returns 0. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with "non-overlapping" but intervals sorted by l; if two intervals are [1,3] and [5,7], r0=3<l1=5. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a possible issue with "coins.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])" if the input list is already sorted but we sort; okay.
